# Lesson 12: Japan in Pictures

*The whole story retold as a sequence of figures, each captioned with what to notice and linked to the lesson that teaches it, ending with the five puzzles solved.*

---

## How to use this

Nothing here is new. It is the same arc you have just read, drawn rather than argued, which makes it useful twice over: as revision once you have finished the lessons, and as something to flick through before you next read a headline about the yen or the Bank of Japan. If a caption does not land, follow the link back to the lesson that builds the idea properly.

---

## Part 1: The machine

*Taught in [Lesson 1](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-01-the-machine)*

### 1. How a BOJ decision travels

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 470" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Flow diagram of how a Bank of Japan interest rate decision travels through the economy. The policy rate, set against the US Federal Reserve rate, feeds three channels. The currency channel runs from the gap between Japanese and US rates to the yen and on to import prices. The demand channel runs from borrowing costs to spending and hiring, then to wages and domestic demand. The bond channel runs from government bond yields to the government interest bill and the balance sheets of banks and insurers, and on to the room the state has to spend. All three meet at inflation and growth, where the target is about 2% inflation driven by wages rather than by a weak yen, and the result feeds back into the next rate decision." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">How a BOJ decision travels</text>

<path d="M540,404 L640,404 L640,84 L486,84" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.4" fill="none" stroke-opacity="0.45" stroke-dasharray="5 4" stroke-linejoin="round"/>

<text x="648" y="250" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.7" transform="rotate(90 648 250)">feedback</text>
<text x="20" y="458" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.6">The BOJ sets the short rate; markets set the yen and long yields; the target is where the channels meet.</text>
</svg>

Three channels (currency, demand and bonds) carry a rate decision to inflation and growth, and the result feeds back into the next decision. The target is 2% inflation driven by wages, not by a weak yen.

### 2. The nominal economy stood still for twenty years

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 360" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of Japan's gross domestic product in trillions of yen, quarterly at an annual rate, 1994 to mid-2026. Nominal GDP, measured in current yen, stayed between about 494 and 555 trillion yen from 1994 to 2013 because modest real growth was cancelled out by gently falling prices. From 2014, and especially after 2022, nominal GDP pulled away, reaching about 689 trillion yen in April to June 2026, while real GDP, measured in 2015 prices, rose more slowly to about 599 trillion." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

Flat nominal GDP meant flat revenues, flat pay and flat tax receipts. Inflation since 2022 is why the red line finally broke away.

### 3. The real rate decides whether money is tight

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart comparing two situations. In a deflation, with a policy rate of 0% and prices falling by 1% a year, the real interest rate is plus 1%, which is tight money. In September 2026, with a policy rate of 1.25% and underlying inflation (excluding fresh food and energy) of 1.9%, the real rate is about -0.7%, still below zero, so policy is still stimulating. The estimated neutral range of 1% to 2.5% in nominal terms is shown for reference." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">The number that matters is the real rate</text>
<text x="20" y="45.5" font-size="11.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.72">Real interest rate = nominal rate minus inflation</text>
<line x1="100" y1="248.4" x2="640" y2="248.4" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="92" y="252.4" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">−1%</text>
<line x1="100" y1="205.3" x2="640" y2="205.3" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.45"/>
<text x="92" y="209.3" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">0%</text>
<line x1="100" y1="162.2" x2="640" y2="162.2" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="92" y="166.2" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">1%</text>
<line x1="100" y1="119.1" x2="640" y2="119.1" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="92" y="123.1" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">2%</text>
<line x1="100" y1="76" x2="640" y2="76" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="92" y="80" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">3%</text>
<text x="220" y="64" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">Deflation trap (illustrative)</text>
<text x="145" y="198.3" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">0%</text>
<text x="145" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Policy rate</text>
<path d="M190,205.3 L190,245.4 Q190,248.4 193,248.4 L247,248.4 Q250,248.4 250,245.4 L250,205.3 Z" class="vf2" fill="#eb6834"><title>Deflation trap (illustrative): Inflation −1.00%</title></path>
<text x="220" y="263.4" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">−1%</text>
<text x="220" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Inflation</text>
<path d="M265,205.3 L265,165.2 Q265,162.2 268,162.2 L322,162.2 Q325,162.2 325,165.2 L325,205.3 Z" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>Deflation trap (illustrative): Real rate 1.00%</title></path>
<text x="295" y="155.2" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">1%</text>
<text x="295" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Real rate</text>
<text x="500" y="64" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">September 2026</text>
<path d="M395,205.3 L395,154.4 Q395,151.4 398,151.4 L452,151.4 Q455,151.4 455,154.4 L455,205.3 Z" class="vf8" fill="#e34948"><title>September 2026: Policy rate 1.25%</title></path>
<text x="425" y="144.4" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">1.25%</text>
<text x="425" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Policy rate</text>
<path d="M470,205.3 L470,125.2 Q470,122.2 473,122.2 L527,122.2 Q530,122.2 530,125.2 L530,205.3 Z" class="vf2" fill="#eb6834"><title>September 2026: Inflation* 1.93%</title></path>
<text x="500" y="115.2" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">1.9%</text>
<text x="500" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Inflation*</text>
<path d="M545,205.3 L545,231.6 Q545,234.6 548,234.6 L602,234.6 Q605,234.6 605,231.6 L605,205.3 Z" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>September 2026: Real rate −0.68%</title></path>
<text x="575" y="249.6" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">−0.7%</text>
<text x="575" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Real rate</text>
<line x1="360" y1="70" x2="360" y2="300" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.2"/>
<text x="20" y="318" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.6">*Underlying inflation: consumer prices excluding fresh food and energy, August 2026 (MIC).</text>
</svg>

Deflation turns a zero rate into tight money. Today's 1.25% is still a negative real rate.

### 4. Interest versus growth

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 300" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Two waterfall charts of how a debt ratio of 200% of GDP changes in a year. On the left, with an average interest rate of 1%, nominal growth of 4% and a primary deficit of 2% of GDP, growth shrinks the ratio by 7.7 points, interest adds 1.9 and the deficit adds 2, so the ratio falls to 196.2%. On the right, with interest of 3% and growth of 2%, growth removes 3.9 points, interest adds 5.9 and the deficit adds 2, so the ratio rises to 204.0%." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">Interest versus growth: the arithmetic of a debt mountain</text>
<text x="20" y="45.5" font-size="11.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.72">Debt of 200% of GDP and a primary deficit of 2% of GDP, under two settings</text>
<text x="200" y="64" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">Interest 1%, growth 4%</text>
<text x="200" y="79" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.75">roughly Japan since 2023</text>

<text x="54" y="209.4" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">190%</text>
<line x1="60" y1="156.9" x2="350" y2="156.9" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="54" y="160.9" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">200%</text>
<line x1="60" y1="108.3" x2="350" y2="108.3" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="54" y="112.3" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">210%</text>
<rect x="68" y="156.9" width="46" height="97.1" rx="2" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>Interest 1%, growth 4%: starting debt 200% of GDP</title></rect>
<text x="91" y="150.9" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">200</text>
<text x="91" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Start</text>
<rect x="126" y="156.9" width="46" height="37.4" rx="2" class="vf3" fill="#1baf7a"><title>Interest 1%, growth 4%: growth effect -7.7 points</title></rect>
<text x="149" y="150.9" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">−7.7</text>
<text x="149" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Growth</text>

<text x="207" y="178.9" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">+1.9</text>
<text x="207" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Interest</text>
<rect x="242" y="175.2" width="46" height="9.7" rx="2" class="vf2" fill="#eb6834"><title>Interest 1%, growth 4%: deficit effect +2.0 points</title></rect>
<text x="265" y="169.2" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">+2.0</text>
<text x="265" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Deficit</text>
<rect x="300" y="175.2" width="46" height="78.8" rx="2" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>Interest 1%, growth 4%: debt a year later 196.2% of GDP</title></rect>
<text x="323" y="169.2" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">196.2</text>
<text x="323" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">A year on</text>
<text x="520" y="64" font-size="12" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">Interest 3%, growth 2%</text>
<text x="520" y="79" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.75">the setting bond markets fear</text>
<line x1="380" y1="254" x2="670" y2="254" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="374" y="258" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">180%</text>
<line x1="380" y1="205.4" x2="670" y2="205.4" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="374" y="209.4" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">190%</text>
<line x1="380" y1="156.9" x2="670" y2="156.9" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="374" y="160.9" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">200%</text>
<line x1="380" y1="108.3" x2="670" y2="108.3" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="374" y="112.3" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.7">210%</text>
<rect x="388" y="156.9" width="46" height="97.1" rx="2" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>Interest 3%, growth 2%: starting debt 200% of GDP</title></rect>
<text x="411" y="150.9" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">200</text>
<text x="411" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Start</text>
<rect x="446" y="156.9" width="46" height="19" rx="2" class="vf3" fill="#1baf7a"><title>Interest 3%, growth 2%: growth effect -3.9 points</title></rect>
<text x="469" y="150.9" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">−3.9</text>
<text x="469" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Growth</text>
<rect x="504" y="147.3" width="46" height="28.6" rx="2" class="vf5" fill="#e87ba4"><title>Interest 3%, growth 2%: interest effect +5.9 points</title></rect>
<text x="527" y="141.3" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">+5.9</text>
<text x="527" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Interest</text>
<rect x="562" y="137.6" width="46" height="9.7" rx="2" class="vf2" fill="#eb6834"><title>Interest 3%, growth 2%: deficit effect +2.0 points</title></rect>
<text x="585" y="131.6" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">+2.0</text>
<text x="585" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">Deficit</text>
<rect x="620" y="137.6" width="46" height="116.4" rx="2" class="vf7" fill="#4a3aa7"><title>Interest 3%, growth 2%: debt a year later 204.0% of GDP</title></rect>
<text x="643" y="131.6" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">204.0</text>
<text x="643" y="270" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.8">A year on</text>
<text x="20" y="292" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.6">Change in debt ratio ≈ debt × (r − g) ÷ (1 + g) + primary deficit. Illustrative numbers; the scale starts at 180%.</text>
</svg>

When nominal growth beats the interest rate, a debt ratio can fall even with a deficit. Reverse the two and it rises.

## Part 2: The long fight against deflation

*Taught in [Lesson 2](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-02-bubble-bust-and-deflation), [Lesson 3](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-03-false-dawn-and-the-super-yen) and [Lesson 4](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-04-abenomics-and-the-bazooka)*

Cheap money after the Plaza Accord tripled the Nikkei; rate rises burst it, and the index fell to a fifth of its peak by 2003.

### 6. How deflation arrived

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of Japan's consumer price inflation, all items, year on year, monthly, 1985 to 2012. Inflation peaked near 4% in 1990 and 1991 at the top of the bubble, slid towards zero through the early 1990s, jumped briefly in 1997 when the consumption tax rose from 3% to 5%, and then turned negative from 1999. For most of the next fourteen years prices fell gently, by up to about 2% a year, apart from a spike in 2008 when oil prices surged." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

Ignore the tax bumps and the trend is a slide from about 3% to below zero by 1999, where it stayed for most of fourteen years.

### 7. The carry trade, and its unwinding

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 470" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Two-panel chart, 2004 to mid-2013. Top panel: the yen per dollar, monthly average. The yen weakened to about 123 per dollar in mid-2007, at the height of the carry trade, then strengthened to a record of about 77 in late 2011 before weakening again in early 2013. Bottom panel: the gap between the US Federal Reserve's policy rate and the Bank of Japan's, in percentage points. It peaked at about 5 points in 2006 and 2007 and collapsed to almost zero after the Fed cut its rate to near zero in December 2008." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

A wide rate gap kept the yen weak; when the Fed cut to zero in 2008, the gap vanished and the yen soared to about 75.

### 8. The end of the dependable trade surplus

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of Japan's balance of trade in goods, in trillions of yen, each year from 1996 to 2025, on a balance-of-payments basis. Japan ran a surplus every year, often of 10 to 15 trillion yen, until 2011, when the shutdown of nuclear plants after the March 2011 earthquake forced huge fuel imports. The balance stayed weak or negative after that, with the largest deficit, 15.5 trillion yen, in 2022 when energy prices soared and the yen fell." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">The end of the dependable trade surplus</text>

Fukushima turned Japan into a huge fuel importer. A country that no longer earns a goods surplus creates less natural demand for its currency.

### 9. The bazooka in one line

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 350" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of the Bank of Japan's total assets in trillions of yen, monthly, 1998 to 2026. Assets were about 158 trillion yen at the end of 2012. Quantitative and qualitative easing from April 2013, expanded in October 2014, multiplied them; Covid-era lending and the defence of the yield cap took them to a peak of about 765 trillion yen in August 2024, roughly 119% of a year's GDP. By August 2026 they had eased to about 645 trillion as bond buying was cut back." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

QQE multiplied the BOJ's balance sheet more than fourfold, to about 120% of GDP at its peak.

### 10. The tax that kept tripping the recovery

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Step chart of Japan's consumption tax rate, 1988 to 2030. It was introduced at 3% in April 1989, raised to 5% in April 1997, to 8% in April 2014 and to 10% in October 2019, when a reduced rate of 8% was kept for food. Under the plan approved in 2026, the rate on food is to fall to 1% for two years from April 2027, returning to 8% in April 2029. Each rise was followed by a fall in spending." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

Each consumption-tax rise was followed by a slump in spending. The 2027 cut on food is the first reduction since the tax was born.

---

## Part 3: From control to exit

*Taught in [Lesson 5](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-05-yield-curve-control), [Lesson 6](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-06-the-yen-breaks) and [Lesson 7](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-07-politics-war-and-a-nervous-bond-market)*

### 11. Yield curve control

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 360" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of the 10-year Japanese government bond yield, daily, 2016 to mid-2024, with the band the Bank of Japan allowed under yield curve control. The yield fell to about minus 0.3% in July 2016. After yield curve control began in September 2016 it was pinned close to zero, within about 0.1 percentage point either side, then within 0.2 from July 2018 and 0.25 from March 2021. In 2022, as global yields rose, it pressed against the 0.25% ceiling until the band was widened to 0.5% in December 2022. In 2023 the cap was loosened to 1%, first as a hard limit and then as a reference, and yield curve control was abandoned in March 2024." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">Yield curve control: a ceiling that kept being raised</text>

The BOJ pinned the 10-year yield near zero for seven years, then raised the ceiling step by step as global yields rose, and abandoned the cap in March 2024.

### 12. A tight labour market, but stubborn prices

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 450" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Two-panel chart, 2010 to 2021. Top: Japan's unemployment rate fell from about 5% in 2010 to 2.2% in 2019, the lowest in decades. Bottom: consumer price inflation excluding food and energy stayed between about minus 1% and plus 1% for almost the whole period, apart from a year when the April 2014 consumption tax rise lifted it, and never exceeded 0.7% from 2016 to 2019. A tight labour market did not produce the inflation a textbook would predict." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">A tight labour market, but stubborn prices</text>

Unemployment fell to about 2.2% with barely any inflation: expectations, labour supply and government price cuts all held prices down.

### 13. The central bank that bought half the market

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of the share of Japanese government bonds (including FILP bonds, excluding Treasury bills) held by the Bank of Japan and by overseas investors, quarterly, 1998 to 2026. The Bank of Japan held about 11% at the end of 2012. Its share climbed steeply after 2013 to a peak of 53.9% in 2023 Q3 and had eased to 46.7% by 2026 Q2. The overseas share stayed in single digits, at 7.9% in the latest quarter." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 14. Inflation returns

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 340" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of Japan's core inflation (consumer prices excluding fresh food) and core-core inflation (also excluding energy), year on year, monthly, January 2021 to August 2026. Core inflation was below zero in 2021, partly because of cuts to mobile phone charges, rose above 2% in April 2022 and peaked at 4.2% in January 2023, the highest since 1981. Core-core inflation peaked later, above 4% in mid-2023, as price rises spread to services. Both were back below 2% by mid-2026, core at 1.7% and core-core at 1.9%, held down by energy subsidies and falling rice prices." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">Inflation returns, from abroad first</text>

Imported first, then spreading to services: core-core inflation rose later and stayed up longer than core.

### 15. The wage breakthrough

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 320" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of the average pay rise agreed in Japan's spring wage negotiations (shunto), as tallied by the Rengo union confederation, 1989 to 2026. Settlements were above 5% from 1989 to 1991, fell through the 1990s, and sat between about 1.6% and 2.2% from 1999 to 2022. They jumped to 3.58% in 2023, then 5.10% in 2024, 5.25% in 2025 and 5.01% in 2026, three straight years above 5% for the first time since 1989 to 1991." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 16. A 34-year round trip

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 340" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of the Nikkei 225 stock index, month-end closes, 1985 to 2026, with a horizontal line at the December 1989 closing peak of 38,916. The index spent 34 years below that line, until it closed above it on 22 February 2024. It then went on to a record close of 72,366 on 25 June 2026 and stood at 69,947 on 5 October 2026." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

The Nikkei passed its 1989 peak in February 2024 on profits and governance, then ran on to a record above 72,000.

### 17. When the carry trade snapped

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 320" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of daily percentage changes in the Nikkei 225, July and August 2024. Most days moved by less than 2%. After the Bank of Japan raised rates on 31 July and weak US jobs data on 2 August, the index fell 5.8% on 2 August and 12.4% on 5 August, its worst day since October 1987, then rebounded 10.2% on 6 August." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

A small hike plus a US jobs scare produced Tokyo's worst day since 1987. Japan can no longer normalise without the world feeling it.

### 18. Six hikes, and the gap is still wide

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 340" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Step chart of policy rates from early 2024 to October 2026. The Bank of Japan raised its rate six times: from minus 0.1% to a range of 0 to 0.1% in March 2024, then to 0.25% in July 2024, 0.5% in January 2025, 0.75% in December 2025, 1% in June 2026 and 1.25% in September 2026. The US Federal Reserve cut its upper bound from 5.5% to 3.75% between September 2024 and late 2025, then raised it to 4% in September 2026, leaving a gap of 2.75 percentage points." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

The BOJ climbed from −0.1% to 1.25%, but the Fed hiked too, and the gap stayed at 2.75 points.

### 19. The bond market wakes up

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 340" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of Japanese government bond yields at 2, 10 and 30 years, daily, 2023 to October 2026. All three rose steadily. The 10-year yield was below 1% for most of 2023, passed 2% in December 2025 and first closed above 3% on 2 September 2026 by the Ministry of Finance's measure, the highest since 1996; on 2 October it was 3.10%. The 30-year yield rose from about 1.6% to about 4.1%, and the 2-year from near zero to about 1.9%." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 20. From fighting a strong yen to fighting a weak one

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 340" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of Japan's currency interventions by calendar year, 1991 to 2026, in trillions of yen. Bars below the line show yen sold to stop it strengthening; bars above show yen bought to support it. Japan sold yen in most years of the 1990s and early 2000s, including a record of about 20 trillion yen in 2003 and 15 in 2004, and again in 2010 and 2011. It bought yen in 1997 and 1998, then not again until 2022 (about 9.2 trillion), 2024 (about 15.3 trillion) and 2026 (about 27.1 trillion, a record)." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 21. Twenty years of the rate gap and the yen

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 480" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Two-panel chart, 2005 to 2026. Top panel: policy rates. The US Federal Reserve rate (top of its range) rose to 5.25% in 2006 and 2007, fell to near zero from 2008 to 2015, rose to 2.5% in 2018 and to 5.5% in 2023, and was 4% in October 2026. The Bank of Japan rate stayed between minus 0.1% and 0.5% for the whole period until 2025 and reached 1.25% in September 2026. Bottom panel: the yen per dollar, monthly average. The yen weakened when the gap between US and Japanese rates was wide, in 2005 to 2007 and from 2022, and strengthened when the gap collapsed after 2008. It averaged about 156 in September 2026." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 22. The slow ramp in interest costs

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 330" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart, 1975 to 2026, of the average interest rate on all outstanding Japanese government bonds (end of each fiscal year) and the yearly average 10-year yield on new bonds. The average rate lags the market yield by years, because only maturing bonds are refinanced. Market yields fell below the average rate in the 1990s and dragged it down from over 7% to 0.76% in fiscal 2022. Since 2023 the 10-year yield has risen above the average rate, to about 2.6% in 2026 so far, so the average has started to climb: 0.98% at the end of fiscal 2025." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">The slow ramp: why interest costs rise for years</text>

<text x="322.6" y="322" font-size="11.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.85">10-year yield, yearly average</text>
<text x="660" y="322" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.6">Source: Ministry of Finance</text>
</svg>

Market yields are now above the average rate on existing debt, so that average will climb for years even if yields stop rising.

### 23. From trading nation to investor nation

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 360" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Stacked bar chart of the components of Japan's current account, trillions of yen, 1996 to 2025, with the total as a line. In the late 1990s and 2000s the surplus came mostly from trade in goods. After 2011 the goods balance shrank or turned negative, services stayed in deficit, and the surplus came overwhelmingly from primary income: returns on Japan's overseas investments, which reached 41.8 trillion yen in 2025. The total surplus was 32.2 trillion yen in 2025." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

The surplus now comes from investment income, much of it reinvested abroad, which is part of why the yen stays weak.

### 24. The yen's real value is back to the 1960s

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 320" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Line chart of the yen's real effective exchange rate, the BIS narrow index adjusted for inflation across trading partners, 2020 = 100, monthly, 1964 to July 2026. The yen's real value rose from about 60 in the mid-1960s to a peak of 180 in April 1995, then fell for three decades. By July 2026 it stood at 62, lower than in any month since 1964 except a few months at the very start of the series." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

### 25. Why Japanese money is tempted home

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 300" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of the choice facing a Japanese life insurer on 2 October 2026. A 30-year Japanese government bond yields 4.15% with no currency risk. A 30-year US Treasury yields 5.61%, but protecting it against currency moves costs roughly the gap between US and Japanese short-term rates, about 4.00% minus 1.23%, or 2.77 percentage points a year. That leaves a currency-hedged return of about 2.84%, below the yen bond." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">
<text x="20" y="28" font-size="14" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">Why Japanese money is tempted home</text>
<text x="20" y="45.5" font-size="11.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.72">A life insurer's 30-year choice, yield % a year</text>
<line x1="90" y1="240" x2="640" y2="240" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.45"/>
<text x="82" y="244" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">0%</text>
<line x1="90" y1="191.4" x2="640" y2="191.4" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="82" y="195.4" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">2%</text>
<line x1="90" y1="142.9" x2="640" y2="142.9" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="82" y="146.8" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">4%</text>
<line x1="90" y1="94.3" x2="640" y2="94.3" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1" stroke-opacity="0.12"/>
<text x="82" y="98.2" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="end" opacity="0.72" style="font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums">6%</text>
<path d="M110,240 L110,142.3 Q110,139.3 113,139.3 L187,139.3 Q190,139.3 190,142.3 L190,240 Z" class="vf3" fill="#1baf7a" fill-opacity="1"><title>30-year JGB: 4.15%</title></path>
<text x="150" y="131.3" font-size="13" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">4.15%</text>
<text x="150" y="260" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">30-year JGB</text>
<text x="150" y="275" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.75">no currency risk</text>
<path d="M290,240 L290,106.8 Q290,103.8 293,103.8 L367,103.8 Q370,103.8 370,106.8 L370,240 Z" class="vf1" fill="#2a78d6" fill-opacity="1"><title>30-year US Treasury: 5.61%</title></path>
<text x="330" y="95.8" font-size="13" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">5.61%</text>
<text x="330" y="260" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">30-year US Treasury</text>
<text x="330" y="275" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.75">unhedged: exposed to the yen</text>
<path d="M470,240 L470,174.1 Q470,171.1 473,171.1 L547,171.1 Q550,171.1 550,174.1 L550,240 Z" class="vf1" fill="#2a78d6" fill-opacity="0.55"><title>US Treasury, hedged: 2.84%</title></path>
<text x="510" y="163.1" font-size="13" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">2.84%</text>
<text x="510" y="260" font-size="11" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="600">US Treasury, hedged</text>
<text x="510" y="275" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="middle" opacity="0.75">after the cost of hedging</text>
<line x1="375" y1="107.8" x2="470" y2="167.1" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-opacity="0.6"/>
<polygon points="470,167.1 463.1,166.1 466.1,161.3" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.6"/>
<text x="442" y="111.8" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" font-weight="600">− 2.77 hedge cost</text>
<text x="20" y="294" font-size="10.5" fill="currentColor" opacity="0.6">Sources: Ministry of Finance; FRED (DGS30, DTB3); BOJ call rate. Hedge cost approximated by the short-rate gap.</text>
</svg>

After hedging, a 30-year JGB beats a 30-year Treasury. A shift home would push up yields in America and Europe.

### 26. The carry trade's asymmetry

<svg viewBox="0 0 680 320" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Bar chart of the profit or loss, in millions of yen, after one year on a carry trade: borrow 100 million yen at 0.5%, convert at 160 yen per dollar into 625,000 dollars, and earn 4% in dollars. Result by year-end exchange rate: at 165, +6.75 million; at 160, +3.50 million; at 155, +0.25 million; at 150, minus 3.00 million; at 145, minus 6.25 million; at 140, minus 9.50 million. The trade breaks even at about 154.6, so a rise in the yen of only about 3.5% wipes out a year of interest." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

Small steady gains, sudden large losses: a 3.5% rise in the yen erases a year of carry.

### 27. Forty years at a glance

<svg viewBox="0 0 760 250" width="100%" role="img" aria-label="Timeline strip from 1985 to 2026 in three rows. Top row: Bank of Japan governors: Sumita to 1989, Mieno 1989 to 1994, Matsushita 1994 to 1998, Hayami 1998 to 2003, Fukui 2003 to 2008, Shirakawa 2008 to 2013, Kuroda 2013 to 2023 and Ueda from 2023. Middle row: policy eras: the bubble to 1989, bust and bad loans in the 1990s, zero rates from 1999, the first quantitative easing 2001 to 2006, the false dawn 2006 to 2008, crisis and a strong yen 2008 to 2012, quantitative and qualitative easing from 2013, negative rates and yield curve control 2016 to 2024, and normalisation from March 2024. Bottom row: the policy rate, which sat at or below 0.5% from 1995 until 2025." fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg">

Eight governors, nine policy eras and a policy rate that sat at or below 0.5% for thirty years.

---

## The five puzzles, solved

**1. How can a country with debt of twice its GDP borrow so cheaply?** Because the debt is in yen and owed at home, Japan saves more than it invests, deflation made low yields rational, and the BOJ became the dominant buyer. Since 2022, nominal growth has outrun the average interest rate, so the ratio is falling. The risk is the slow ramp as old bonds are refinanced at higher yields. ([Lessons 1](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-01-the-machine) and [9](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-09-the-big-debates))

**2. Why did the stock market take 34 years to regain its peak?** Because the 1989 peak was a bubble built on cheap credit and land. Its collapse left a balance sheet recession, zombie firms and deflation. The recovery came only with real profits, a weak yen and a governance push that made companies return cash to shareholders. ([Lessons 2](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-02-bubble-bust-and-deflation) and [6](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-06-the-yen-breaks))

**3. How can a safe-haven currency sink to 40-year lows?** The safe-haven reflex still works in panics, because Japanese money comes home. But in normal times the yen is driven by the rate gap with America, which has been wide since 2022, and by structural outflows: energy imports, the digital deficit, reinvested investment income and households buying foreign shares. ([Lessons 3](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-03-false-dawn-and-the-super-yen), [6](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-06-the-yen-breaks) and [9](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-09-the-big-debates))

**4. Why did the lost decades never bring mass unemployment?** Because the working-age population was shrinking, firms protected permanent jobs while cutting pay growth and hiring cheaper non-regular staff, and stagnation showed up as frozen prices and wages rather than layoffs. Unemployment peaked at about 5.5%. ([Lessons 1](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-01-the-machine), [2](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-02-bubble-bust-and-deflation) and [5](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-05-yield-curve-control))

**5. Why did the yen fall when the BOJ raised rates in September 2026?** Because the Fed had raised its own rate two days earlier, so the gap between them did not narrow at all. The yen follows the gap, not Japan's rate on its own. ([Lesson 7](?slug=japan-economy&lesson=lesson-07-politics-war-and-a-nervous-bond-market))
